<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Suriname" title="Geography of Suriname">Geography of Suriname</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:View_of_Brokopondo_Reservoir_(33537723975).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/View_of_Brokopondo_Reservoir_%2833537723975%29.jpg/220px-View_of_Brokopondo_Reservoir_%2833537723975%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/View_of_Brokopondo_Reservoir_%2833537723975%29.jpg/330px-View_of_Brokopondo_Reservoir_%2833537723975%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/View_of_Brokopondo_Reservoir_%2833537723975%29.jpg/440px-View_of_Brokopondo_Reservoir_%2833537723975%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4896" data-file-height="2752" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Brokopondo_Reservoir" title="Brokopondo Reservoir">Brokopondo Reservoir</a> surrounded by tropical rainforest</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Morning_fog_(2719242329).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Morning_fog_%282719242329%29.jpg/220px-Morning_fog_%282719242329%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Morning_fog_%282719242329%29.jpg/330px-Morning_fog_%282719242329%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Morning_fog_%282719242329%29.jpg/440px-Morning_fog_%282719242329%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="819" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Coppename_River" title="Coppename River">Coppename river</a>, one of many rivers in the interior</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Leatherback_Turtle_near_Galibi.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Leatherback_Turtle_near_Galibi.jpg/220px-Leatherback_Turtle_near_Galibi.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Leatherback_Turtle_near_Galibi.jpg/330px-Leatherback_Turtle_near_Galibi.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Leatherback_Turtle_near_Galibi.jpg/440px-Leatherback_Turtle_near_Galibi.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1704" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Leatherback_sea_turtle" title="Leatherback sea turtle">Leatherback sea turtle</a> on the beach near the village of Galibi</figcaption></figure>
<p>Suriname is the smallest independent country in South America. Situated on the <a href="/wiki/Guiana_Shield" title="Guiana Shield">Guiana Shield</a>, it lies mostly between latitudes <a href="/wiki/1st_parallel_north" title="1st parallel north">1°</a> and <a href="/wiki/6th_parallel_north" title="6th parallel north">6°N</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/54th_meridian_west" title="54th meridian west">54°</a> and <a href="/wiki/58th_meridian_west" title="58th meridian west">58°W</a>. The country can be divided into two main geographic regions. The northern, lowland coastal area (roughly above the line Albina-Paranam-Wageningen) has been cultivated, and most of the population lives here. The southern part consists of tropical <a href="/wiki/Rainforest" title="Rainforest">rainforest</a> and sparsely inhabited <a href="/wiki/Savanna" title="Savanna">savanna</a> along the border with Brazil, covering about 80% of Suriname's land surface.
</p><p>The two main mountain ranges are the <a href="/wiki/Bakhuys_Mountains" class="mw-redirect" title="Bakhuys Mountains">Bakhuys Mountains</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Van_Asch_Van_Wijck_Mountains" title="Van Asch Van Wijck Mountains">Van Asch Van Wijck Mountains</a>. <a href="/wiki/Julianatop" title="Julianatop">Julianatop</a> is the highest mountain in the country at 1,286 metres (4,219&#160;ft) above sea level. Other mountains include <a href="/wiki/Tafelberg,_Suriname" title="Tafelberg, Suriname">Tafelberg</a> at 1,026 metres (3,366&#160;ft), Mount <a href="/wiki/Kasikasima" title="Kasikasima">Kasikasima</a> at 718 metres (2,356&#160;ft), Goliathberg at 358 metres (1,175&#160;ft) and <a href="/wiki/Voltzberg" title="Voltzberg">Voltzberg</a> at 240 metres (790&#160;ft).
</p><p>Suriname contains six terrestrial ecoregions: <a href="/wiki/Guayanan_Highlands_moist_forests" class="mw-redirect" title="Guayanan Highlands moist forests">Guayanan Highlands moist forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guianan_moist_forests" title="Guianan moist forests">Guianan moist forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Paramaribo_swamp_forests" title="Paramaribo swamp forests">Paramaribo swamp forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tepuis" class="mw-redirect" title="Tepuis">Tepuis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guianan_savanna" title="Guianan savanna">Guianan savanna</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Guianan_mangroves" title="Guianan mangroves">Guianan mangroves</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Its <a href="/wiki/Forest_cover" title="Forest cover">forest cover</a> is 90.2%, the highest of any nation in the world. The country had a 2019 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 9.39/10, ranking it fifth globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Borders">Borders</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Borders">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Borders_of_Suriname" title="Borders of Suriname">Borders of Suriname</a></div>
<p>Suriname is situated between <a href="/wiki/French_Guiana" title="French Guiana">French Guiana</a> to the east and <a href="/wiki/Guyana" title="Guyana">Guyana</a> to the west. The southern border is shared with <a href="/wiki/Brazil" title="Brazil">Brazil</a> and the northern border is the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Ocean" title="Atlantic Ocean">Atlantic</a> coast. The southernmost borders with French Guiana and Guyana are disputed by these countries along the <a href="/wiki/Maroni_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Maroni River">Marowijne</a> and <a href="/wiki/Courantyne_River" title="Courantyne River">Corantijn</a> rivers, respectively, while a part of the disputed <a href="/wiki/Maritime_boundary" title="Maritime boundary">maritime boundary</a> with Guyana was arbitrated by the <a href="/wiki/Permanent_Court_of_Arbitration" title="Permanent Court of Arbitration">Permanent Court of Arbitration</a> convened under the rules set out in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/United_Nations_Convention_on_the_Law_of_the_Sea/Annex_VII" class="extiw" title="s:United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea/Annex VII">Annex VII</a> of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Convention_on_the_Law_of_the_Sea" title="United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea">United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea</a> on 20 September 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Climate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Lying <a href="/wiki/2nd_parallel_north" title="2nd parallel north">two</a> to <a href="/wiki/5th_parallel_north" title="5th parallel north">five degrees north</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Equator" title="Equator">equator</a>, Suriname has a very hot and wet <a href="/wiki/Tropical_climate" title="Tropical climate">tropical climate</a>, and temperatures do not vary much throughout the year. Average relative humidity is between 80% and 90%. Its average temperature ranges from 29 to 34&#160;°C (84 to 93&#160;°F). Due to the high humidity, actual temperatures are distorted and may therefore feel up to 6&#160;°C (11&#160;°F) hotter than the recorded temperature.
</p><p>The year has two <a href="/wiki/Wet_season" title="Wet season">wet seasons</a>, from April to August and from November to February. It also has two <a href="/wiki/Dry_season" title="Dry season">dry seasons</a>, from August to November and February to April.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Seasons_in_Suriname_original.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Seasons_in_Suriname_original.png/220px-Seasons_in_Suriname_original.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Seasons_in_Suriname_original.png/330px-Seasons_in_Suriname_original.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Seasons_in_Suriname_original.png/440px-Seasons_in_Suriname_original.png 2x" data-file-width="7834" data-file-height="6343" /></a><figcaption>Seasons in Suriname</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Suriname" title="Climate change in Suriname">Climate change in Suriname</a> is leading to warmer temperatures and more <a href="/wiki/Extreme_weather" title="Extreme weather">extreme weather</a> events. As a relatively poor country, its contributions to global <a href="/wiki/Climate_change" title="Climate change">climate change</a> have been limited. Because of the large forest cover, the country has been running a <a href="/wiki/Carbon_dioxide_removal" title="Carbon dioxide removal">carbon negative</a> economy since 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Biodiversity_and_conservation">Biodiversity and conservation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Biodiversity and conservation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dendrobates_azureus_(Dendrobates_tinctorius)_Edit.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Dendrobates_azureus_%28Dendrobates_tinctorius%29_Edit.jpg/220px-Dendrobates_azureus_%28Dendrobates_tinctorius%29_Edit.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="170" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Dendrobates_azureus_%28Dendrobates_tinctorius%29_Edit.jpg/330px-Dendrobates_azureus_%28Dendrobates_tinctorius%29_Edit.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Dendrobates_azureus_%28Dendrobates_tinctorius%29_Edit.jpg/440px-Dendrobates_azureus_%28Dendrobates_tinctorius%29_Edit.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2591" data-file-height="2002" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Blue_poison_dart_frog" title="Blue poison dart frog">blue poison dart frog</a> is endemic to Suriname.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Biodiversity_in_Suriname" title="Biodiversity in Suriname">Biodiversity in Suriname</a></div>
<p>Due to the variety of habitats and temperatures, biodiversity in Suriname is considered high.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In October 2013, 16 international scientists researching the ecosystems during a three-week expedition in Suriname's <a href="/w/index.php?title=Upper_Palumeu_River&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Upper Palumeu River (page does not exist)">Upper Palumeu River</a> Watershed catalogued 1,378 species and found 60—including six frogs, one snake, and 11 fish—that may be previously unknown species.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> According to the environmental non-profit <a href="/wiki/Conservation_International" title="Conservation International">Conservation International</a>, which funded the expedition, Suriname's ample supply of fresh water is vital to the biodiversity and healthy ecosystems of the region.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Snakewood (<i><a href="/wiki/Brosimum_guianense" title="Brosimum guianense">Brosimum guianense</a></i>), a tree, is native to this tropical region of the Americas. Customs in Suriname report that snakewood is often illegally exported to French Guiana, thought to be for the crafts industry.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 21 March 2013, Suriname's REDD+ Readiness Preparation Proposal (R-PP 2013) was approved by the member countries of the Participants Committee of the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Forest_Carbon_Partnership_Facility&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (page does not exist)">Forest Carbon Partnership Facility</a> (FCPF).<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As in other parts of Central and South America, indigenous communities have increased their activism to protect their lands and preserve habitat. In March 2015, the "<a href="/wiki/Trio_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Trio people">Trio</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wayana_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Wayana people">Wayana</a> communities presented a declaration of cooperation to the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Suriname" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assembly of Suriname">National Assembly of Suriname</a> that announces an indigenous conservation corridor spanning 72,000 square kilometers (27,799 square miles) of southern Suriname. The declaration, led by these indigenous communities and with the support of <a href="/wiki/Conservation_International" title="Conservation International">Conservation International</a> (CI) and <a href="/wiki/World_Wildlife_Fund" class="mw-redirect" title="World Wildlife Fund">World Wildlife Fund</a> <a href="/wiki/WWF_Guianas" title="WWF Guianas">(WWF) Guianas</a>, comprises almost half of the total area of Suriname.<sup id="cite_ref-guardians_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guardians-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> This area includes large forests and is considered "essential for the country's <a href="/wiki/Climate_resilience" title="Climate resilience">climate resilience</a>, freshwater security, and green development strategy."
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Central_Suriname_Nature_Reserve" title="Central Suriname Nature Reserve">Central Suriname Nature Reserve</a> has been designated a UNESCO <a href="/wiki/World_Heritage_Site" title="World Heritage Site">World Heritage Site</a> for its unspoiled forests and <a href="/wiki/Biodiversity" title="Biodiversity">biodiversity</a>. There are many national parks in the country including Galibi National Reserve along the coast; <a href="/wiki/Brownsberg_Nature_Park" title="Brownsberg Nature Park">Brownsberg Nature Park</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eilerts_de_Haan_Nature_Park" class="mw-redirect" title="Eilerts de Haan Nature Park">Eilerts de Haan Nature Park</a> in central Suriname; and the <a href="/wiki/Sipaliwani_Nature_Reserve" class="mw-redirect" title="Sipaliwani Nature Reserve">Sipaliwani Nature Reserve</a> on the Brazilian border. In all, 16% of the country's land area is national parks and lakes, according to the UNEP World Conservation Monitoring Centre.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Suriname" title="Economy of Suriname">Economy of Suriname</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Suriname_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Suriname_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/220px-Suriname_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="143" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Suriname_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/330px-Suriname_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Suriname_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/440px-Suriname_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a><figcaption>A proportional representation of Suriname exports, 2019</figcaption></figure>
<p>Suriname's democracy gained some strength after the turbulent 1990s, and its economy became more diversified and less dependent on Dutch financial assistance. <a href="/wiki/Bauxite" title="Bauxite">Bauxite</a> (aluminium ore) mining used to be a strong revenue source but has ended now. The discovery and exploitation of oil and gold has added substantially to Suriname's economic independence. Agriculture, especially rice and bananas, remains a strong component of the economy, and <a href="/wiki/Ecotourism" title="Ecotourism">ecotourism</a> is providing new economic opportunities. More than 93% of Suriname's landmass consists of unspoiled rainforest. With the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Central_Suriname_Nature_Reserve" title="Central Suriname Nature Reserve">Central Suriname Nature Reserve</a> in 1998, Suriname signaled its commitment to the conservation of this precious resource. The Central Suriname Nature Reserve became a <a href="/wiki/World_Heritage_Site" title="World Heritage Site">World Heritage Site</a> in 2000.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Financien.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Financien.JPG/220px-Financien.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="122" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Financien.JPG/330px-Financien.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Financien.JPG/440px-Financien.JPG 2x" data-file-width="702" data-file-height="389" /></a><figcaption>Ministry of Finance</figcaption></figure>
<p>The economy of Suriname was dominated by the bauxite industry, which accounted for more than 15% of GDP and 70% of export earnings up to 2016. Other main export products include rice, bananas, and <a href="/wiki/Shrimp" title="Shrimp">shrimp</a>. Suriname has recently started exploiting some of its sizeable <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">oil</a><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> and gold<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> reserves. About a quarter of the people work in the agricultural sector. The Surinamese economy is very dependent on commerce, its main trade partners being the Netherlands, the United States, Canada, and <a href="/wiki/Caribbean" title="Caribbean">Caribbean</a> countries, mainly <a href="/wiki/Trinidad_and_Tobago" title="Trinidad and Tobago">Trinidad and Tobago</a> and the islands of the former <a href="/wiki/Netherlands_Antilles" title="Netherlands Antilles">Netherlands Antilles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After assuming power in the fall of 1996, the <a href="/wiki/Jules_Wijdenbosch" title="Jules Wijdenbosch">Wijdenbosch</a> government ended the <a href="/wiki/Structural_adjustment" title="Structural adjustment">structural adjustment</a> program of the previous government, claiming it was unfair to the poorer elements of society. Tax revenues fell as old taxes lapsed and the government failed to implement new tax alternatives. By the end of 1997, the allocation of new Dutch development funds was frozen as Surinamese Government relations with the Netherlands deteriorated. Economic growth slowed in 1998, with decline in the mining, construction, and <a href="/wiki/Utility" title="Utility">utility</a> sectors. Rampant government expenditures, poor tax collection, a bloated civil service, and reduced foreign aid in 1999 contributed to the fiscal deficit, estimated at 11% of GDP. The government sought to cover this deficit through monetary expansion, which led to a dramatic increase in inflation.  It takes longer on average to register a new business in Suriname than virtually any other country in the world (694 days or about 99 weeks).<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>GDP (2010 est.): US$4.794 <a href="/wiki/Billion" title="Billion">billion</a>.</li>
<li>Annual growth rate real GDP (2010 est.): 3.5%.</li>
<li>Per capita GDP (2010 est.): US$9,900.</li>
<li>Inflation (2007): 6.4%.</li>
<li>Natural resources: Bauxite, gold, oil, iron ore, other minerals; forests; hydroelectric potential; fish and shrimp.</li>
<li>Agriculture: Products—rice, bananas, timber, palm kernels, coconuts, peanuts, citrus fruits, and forest products.</li>
<li>Industry: Types—alumina, oil, gold, fish, shrimp, lumber.</li>
<li>Trade:
<ul><li>Exports (2012): US$2.563 billion: alumina, gold, crude oil, lumber, shrimp and fish, rice, bananas. Major consumers: US 26.1%, Belgium 17.6%, UAE 12.1%, Canada 10.4%, Guyana 6.5%, France 5.6%, Barbados 4.7%.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_16-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Imports (2012): US$1.782 billion: capital equipment, petroleum, foodstuffs, cotton, consumer goods. Major suppliers: US 25.8%, Netherlands 15.8%, China 9.8%, UAE 7.9%, Antigua and Barbuda 7.3%, Netherlands Antilles 5.4%, Japan 4.2%.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_16-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup></li></ul></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Suriname" title="Demographics of Suriname">Demographics of Suriname</a> and <a href="/wiki/Surinamese_people" title="Surinamese people">Surinamese people</a></div>

<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Suriname_demography.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Suriname_demography.png/220px-Suriname_demography.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="129" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Suriname_demography.png/330px-Suriname_demography.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Suriname_demography.png/440px-Suriname_demography.png 2x" data-file-width="724" data-file-height="426" /></a><figcaption>The population of Suriname from 1961 to 2003, in units of 1000. The slowdown and decline in population growth ~1969–1985 reflects a mass migration to the Netherlands and French Guiana.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 2022, Suriname had a population of roughly 618,040 according to estimates by the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> This compares to 541,638 inhabitants from the 2012 census.<sup id="cite_ref-statistics-suriname1_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-statistics-suriname1-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The Surinamese populace is characterized by high levels of diversity, wherein no particular demographic group constitutes a majority. This is a legacy of centuries of Dutch rule, which entailed successive periods of forced, contracted, or voluntary migration by various nationalities and ethnic groups from around the world.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Population_majorities_per_resort_in_Suriname.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Ethnic groups of Surinme" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Population_majorities_per_resort_in_Suriname.svg/220px-Population_majorities_per_resort_in_Suriname.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Population_majorities_per_resort_in_Suriname.svg/330px-Population_majorities_per_resort_in_Suriname.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Population_majorities_per_resort_in_Suriname.svg/440px-Population_majorities_per_resort_in_Suriname.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="842" data-file-height="595" /></a><figcaption>Ethnic groups of Suriname <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#660000; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;Creoles</div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#0f4d18; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;Hindus</div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#fffc00; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;Maroons</div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#ff0000; </div>
</div>
<p>The largest ethnic group are <a href="/wiki/Indo-Surinamese" title="Indo-Surinamese">Indians</a>, who form over a quarter of the population (27.4%).<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Census3_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Census3-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-statistics-suriname12_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-statistics-suriname12-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The vast majority are descendants of 19th-century indentured workers from India, hailing mostly from <a href="/wiki/Bhojpuri_language" title="Bhojpuri language">Bhojpuri</a> speaking areas of modern <a href="/wiki/Bihar" title="Bihar">Bihar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jharkhand" title="Jharkhand">Jharkhand</a>, and northeastern <a href="/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh" title="Uttar Pradesh">Uttar Pradesh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Haryana" title="Haryana">Haryana</a> and southeastern <a href="/wiki/Tamil_Nadu" title="Tamil Nadu">Tamil Nadu</a>. If counted as one ethnic group, the <a href="/wiki/Afro-Surinamese" title="Afro-Surinamese">Afro-Surinamese</a> are the largest community, at around 37.4%; however, they are usually divided into two cultural/ethnic groups: the <a href="/wiki/Creole_peoples" title="Creole peoples">Creoles</a> and the Maroons. Surinamese Maroons, whose ancestors are mostly runaway slaves that fled to the interior, comprise 21.7% of the population. They are divided into six tribes: <a href="/wiki/Ndyuka_people" title="Ndyuka people">Ndyuka</a> (Aucans), <a href="/wiki/Saramaka" title="Saramaka">Saramaccans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Paramaccan" class="mw-redirect" title="Paramaccan">Paramaccans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kwinti" class="mw-redirect" title="Kwinti">Kwinti</a>, <a href="/wiki/Aluku" title="Aluku">Aluku</a> (Boni) and <a href="/wiki/Matawai_people" title="Matawai people">Matawai</a>. Surinamese <a href="/wiki/Creole_peoples" title="Creole peoples">Creoles</a>, mixed people descending from African slaves and Europeans (mostly Dutch), form 15.7% of the population. <a href="/wiki/Javanese_Surinamese" title="Javanese Surinamese">Javanese</a> make up 14% of the population, and like the East Indians, descend largely from workers contracted from the island of <a href="/wiki/Java" title="Java">Java</a> in the former <a href="/wiki/Dutch_East_Indies" title="Dutch East Indies">Dutch East Indies</a> (modern <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-javanese_in_suriname_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-javanese_in_suriname-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> 13.4% of the population identifies as being of mixed ethnic heritage. <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Surinamese" title="Chinese Surinamese">Chinese</a>, originating from 19th-century indentured workers and some recent migration, make up 7.3% of the population.
</p><p>Other groups include <a href="/wiki/Lebanese_people" title="Lebanese people">Lebanese</a>, primarily <a href="/wiki/Maronites" title="Maronites">Maronites</a>, and <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Suriname" title="History of the Jews in Suriname">Jews</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sephardi_Jews" class="mw-redirect" title="Sephardi Jews">Sephardic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews" title="Ashkenazi Jews">Ashkenazi</a> origin, whose center of population was <a href="/wiki/Jodensavanne" title="Jodensavanne">Jodensavanne</a>. Various <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">indigenous peoples</a> make up 3.7% of the population, with the main groups being the <a href="/wiki/Akurio_people" title="Akurio people">Akurio</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arawak" title="Arawak">Arawak</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kalina_people" title="Kalina people">Kalina</a> (Caribs), <a href="/wiki/Tiriy%C3%B3_people" title="Tiriyó people">Tiriyó</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wayana_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Wayana people">Wayana</a>. They live mainly in the districts of <a href="/wiki/Paramaribo" title="Paramaribo">Paramaribo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wanica_District" title="Wanica District">Wanica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Para_District" title="Para District">Para</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marowijne_District" title="Marowijne District">Marowijne</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sipaliwini_District" title="Sipaliwini District">Sipaliwini</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> A small but influential number of <a href="/wiki/White_Surinamese" title="White Surinamese">Europeans</a> remain in the country, comprising about 1% of the population. They are descended mostly from <a href="/wiki/Dutch_people" title="Dutch people">Dutch</a> 19th-century immigrant farmers, known as "<a href="/wiki/Dutch_Surinamese" title="Dutch Surinamese">Boeroes</a>" (derived from <i>boer</i>, the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_language" title="Dutch language">Dutch</a> word for "farmer"), and to a lesser degree other European groups, such as <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_people" title="Portuguese people">Portuguese</a>. Many Boeroes <a href="/wiki/White_flight" title="White flight">left after independence in 1975</a>.
</p><p>More recently Suriname has seen a new wave of immigrants, namely <a href="/wiki/Brazilians_in_Suriname" title="Brazilians in Suriname">Brazilians</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Surinamese" title="Chinese Surinamese">Chinese</a> (many of them laborers mining for gold). Most do not have legal status.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The vast majority of Suriname's inhabitants (about 90%) live in <a href="/wiki/Paramaribo" title="Paramaribo">Paramaribo</a> or on the coast.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Emigration">Emigration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Emigration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tropenmuseum_Royal_Tropical_Institute_Objectnumber_60008924_Een_groep_Brits-Indische_immigranten.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Tropenmuseum_Royal_Tropical_Institute_Objectnumber_60008924_Een_groep_Brits-Indische_immigranten.jpg/220px-Tropenmuseum_Royal_Tropical_Institute_Objectnumber_60008924_Een_groep_Brits-Indische_immigranten.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Tropenmuseum_Royal_Tropical_Institute_Objectnumber_60008924_Een_groep_Brits-Indische_immigranten.jpg/330px-Tropenmuseum_Royal_Tropical_Institute_Objectnumber_60008924_Een_groep_Brits-Indische_immigranten.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Tropenmuseum_Royal_Tropical_Institute_Objectnumber_60008924_Een_groep_Brits-Indische_immigranten.jpg/440px-Tropenmuseum_Royal_Tropical_Institute_Objectnumber_60008924_Een_groep_Brits-Indische_immigranten.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="548" /></a><figcaption>Immigrants from India</figcaption></figure> 
<p>The option to choose between Surinamese or Dutch citizenship in the years leading up to Suriname's independence in 1975 led to a mass migration to the Netherlands. This migration continued in the period immediately after independence and during military rule in the 1980s and for largely economic reasons extended throughout the 1990s. The <a href="/wiki/Surinamese_people_in_the_Netherlands" title="Surinamese people in the Netherlands">Surinamese community</a> in the Netherlands numbered 350,300 as of 2013<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> (including children and grandchildren of Suriname migrants born in the Netherlands), compared to approximately 566,000 Surinamese in Suriname itself.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_16-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the <a href="/wiki/International_Organization_for_Migration" title="International Organization for Migration">International Organization for Migration</a>, around 272,600 people from Suriname lived in other countries in the late 2010s, in particular in the Netherlands (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;192,000</span>), France (c. 25,000, most of them in <a href="/wiki/French_Guiana" title="French Guiana">French Guiana</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> the United States (c. 15,000), <a href="/wiki/Guyana" title="Guyana">Guyana</a> (c. 5,000), <a href="/wiki/Aruba" title="Aruba">Aruba</a> (c. 1,500), and Canada (c. 1,000).<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Suriname" title="Religion in Suriname">Religion in Suriname</a></div>
<div class="barbox tright" style="overflow-x: auto;">

</tbody></table>
</div>
</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MoscheeSynagoge.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/MoscheeSynagoge.jpg/220px-MoscheeSynagoge.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/MoscheeSynagoge.jpg/330px-MoscheeSynagoge.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/MoscheeSynagoge.jpg/440px-MoscheeSynagoge.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="1944" /></a><figcaption>Synagogue and mosque adjacent to each other in Paramaribo</figcaption></figure>
<p>Suriname's religious makeup is heterogeneous and reflective of the country's multicultural character.<sup id="cite_ref-Census2_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Census2-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-statistics-suriname12_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-statistics-suriname12-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Pew_Research_Center" title="Pew Research Center">Pew research</a> from 2012, Christians are the largest religious community, at slightly over half the population (51.6%), followed by <a href="/wiki/Hinduism" title="Hinduism">Hindus</a> (19.8%) and <a href="/wiki/Muslims" title="Muslims">Muslims</a> (15.2%); other religious minorities include adherents of various folk traditions (5.3%), Buddhists (&lt;1%), <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a> (&lt;1%), practitioners of other faiths (1.8%), and <a href="/wiki/Irreligion" title="Irreligion">unaffiliated</a> (5.4%).<sup id="cite_ref-grf_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-grf-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the 2020 census, 52.3% of Surinamese were Christians; 26.7% were <a href="/wiki/Protestants" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestants">Protestants</a> (11.18% <a href="/wiki/Pentecostal" class="mw-redirect" title="Pentecostal">Pentecostal</a>, 11.16% <a href="/wiki/Moravian_Church" title="Moravian Church">Moravian</a>, 0.7% <a href="/wiki/Calvinism" title="Calvinism">Reformed</a> (including <a href="/wiki/Remonstrants" title="Remonstrants">Remonstrants</a>), and 4.4% other Protestant denominations), while 21.6% were <a href="/wiki/Catholics" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholics">Catholics</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CENSUS2012_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CENSUS2012-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Hindus are the second largest religious group in Suriname, comprising nearly one-fifth of the population (18.8% in 2020),<sup id="cite_ref-grf_94-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-grf-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> the third largest proportion of any country in the Western Hemisphere, after <a href="/wiki/Guyana" title="Guyana">Guyana</a> and <a href="/wiki/Trinidad_and_Tobago" title="Trinidad and Tobago">Trinidad and Tobago</a>, both of which also have large proportions of <a href="/wiki/Indo-Caribbeans" title="Indo-Caribbeans">Indians</a>. Likewise, almost all practitioners of Hinduism are found among the Indo-Surinamese population. Muslims constitute 14.3% of the population, the highest proportion of Muslims in the Americas. They are largely of Javanese or Indian descent.<sup id="cite_ref-CENSUS2012_97-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CENSUS2012-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Folk religions are practiced by 5.6% of the population and include <a href="/wiki/Winti" title="Winti">Winti</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Afro-American_religion" class="mw-redirect" title="Afro-American religion">Afro-American religion</a> practiced mostly by those of Maroon ancestry, <a href="/wiki/Kejaw%C3%A8n" title="Kejawèn">Javanism</a> (0.8%),<sup id="cite_ref-CENSUS2012_97-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CENSUS2012-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> a syncretic faith found among some Javanese Surinamese, and various indigenous folk traditions that are often incorporated into one of the larger religions (usually Christianity). In the 2020 census, 6.2% of the population declared they had "no religion", while a further 1.9% adhere to "other religions".<sup id="cite_ref-grf_94-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-grf-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Butcher_Paramaribo_market.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Butcher_Paramaribo_market.jpg/170px-Butcher_Paramaribo_market.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Butcher_Paramaribo_market.jpg/255px-Butcher_Paramaribo_market.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Butcher_Paramaribo_market.jpg/340px-Butcher_Paramaribo_market.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2112" data-file-height="2816" /></a><figcaption>Butcher in the <a href="/wiki/Central_Market_(Paramaribo)" title="Central Market (Paramaribo)">Central Market</a> in Paramaribo with signs written in <a href="/wiki/Dutch_language" title="Dutch language">Dutch</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Suriname has roughly 14 local languages, but <a href="/wiki/Dutch_language" title="Dutch language">Dutch</a> (<i>Nederlands</i>) is the sole official language and is the language used in education, government, business, and the media.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_16-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Over 60% of the population are <a href="/wiki/First_language" title="First language">native speakers</a> of Dutch<sup id="cite_ref-taalgebied_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-taalgebied-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> and around 20%-30% speak it as a second language. In 2004, Suriname became an associate member of the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Language_Union" title="Dutch Language Union">Dutch Language Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Suriname is one of three Dutch-speaking sovereign countries in the world (the others being the <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a> and <a href="/wiki/Belgium" title="Belgium">Belgium</a>). It is also the only area in the Americas where Dutch is spoken by a majority of the population (as the Caribbean territories in the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_the_Netherlands" title="Kingdom of the Netherlands">Kingdom of the Netherlands</a> all have other majority languages). Finally, Suriname and English-speaking <a href="/wiki/Guyana" title="Guyana">Guyana</a> are the only countries in <a href="/wiki/South_America" title="South America">South America</a> along with English-speaking British dependent territory <a href="/wiki/Falkland_Islands" title="Falkland Islands">Falkland Islands</a> where a <a href="/wiki/Romance_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Romance language">Romance language</a> does not predominate.
</p><p>In Paramaribo, Dutch is the main home language in two-thirds of the households.<sup id="cite_ref-Census_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Census-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The recognition of <i>"Surinaams-Nederlands"</i> ("<a href="/wiki/Surinamese_Dutch" title="Surinamese Dutch">Surinamese Dutch</a>") as a national dialect equal to <i>"Nederlands-Nederlands"</i> ("Dutch Dutch") and <i>"Vlaams-Nederlands"</i> ("Flemish Dutch") was expressed in 2009 by the publication of the <i>Woordenboek Surinaams Nederlands</i> (<i>Surinamese–Dutch Dictionary</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> It is the most commonly spoken language in urban areas. Only in the interior of Suriname (namely parts of <a href="/wiki/Sipaliwini_District" title="Sipaliwini District">Sipaliwini</a> and <a href="/wiki/Brokopondo_District" title="Brokopondo District">Brokopondo</a>) is Dutch seldom spoken.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Sranan_Tongo" title="Sranan Tongo">Sranan Tongo</a>, a local English-based <a href="/wiki/Creole_language" title="Creole language">creole language</a>, is the most widely used <a href="/wiki/Vernacular_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Vernacular language">vernacular language</a> in daily life and business. Together with Dutch, it is considered to be one of the two principal languages of Surinamese <a href="/wiki/Diglossia" title="Diglossia">diglossia</a>. Both are further influenced by other spoken languages which are spoken primarily within ethnic communities. Sranan Tongo is often used interchangeably with Dutch depending on the formality of the setting; Dutch is seen as a <a href="/wiki/Prestige_dialect" class="mw-redirect" title="Prestige dialect">prestige dialect</a> and Sranan Tongo the common <a href="/wiki/Vernacular" title="Vernacular">vernacular</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT2008_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT2008-101">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Caribbean_Hindustani#Sarnami_Hindustani" title="Caribbean Hindustani">Sarnami</a>, a fusion of <a href="/wiki/Bhojpuri_language" title="Bhojpuri language">Bhojpuri</a> and <a href="/wiki/Awadhi_language" title="Awadhi language">Awadhi</a>, is the third-most used language. It is primarily spoken by the descendants of Indian indentured labourers from the former <a href="/wiki/British_Raj" title="British Raj">British India</a>.
</p><p>The six <a href="/wiki/Maroon_(people)" class="mw-redirect" title="Maroon (people)">Maroon</a> languages of Suriname are also considered English-based creole languages, and include <a href="/wiki/Saramaccan_language" title="Saramaccan language">Saramaccan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ndyuka_(language)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ndyuka (language)">Aukan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Aluku" title="Aluku">Aluku</a>, <a href="/wiki/Paramakans" class="mw-redirect" title="Paramakans">Paramaccan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Matawai_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Matawai language">Matawai</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kwinti_language" title="Kwinti language">Kwinti</a>. Aluku, Paramaccan, and Kwinti are so mutually intelligible with Aukan that they can be considered dialects of the Aukan language. The same can be said about Matawai, which is mutually intelligible with Saramaka.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Javanese_language" title="Javanese language">Javanese</a> is used by the descendants of the <a href="/wiki/Javanese_people" title="Javanese people">Javanese people</a>, which were indentured laborers sent from the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_East_Indies" title="Dutch East Indies">Dutch East Indies</a> (now <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>).
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">Amerindian</a> languages include <a href="/wiki/Akurio_language" title="Akurio language">Akurio</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arawak_language" title="Arawak language">Arawak-Lokono</a>, <a href="/wiki/Carib_language" title="Carib language">Carib-Kari'nja</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sikiana_language" title="Sikiana language">Sikiana-Kashuyana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tiriy%C3%B3_language" title="Tiriyó language">Tiro-Tiriyó</a>, <a href="/wiki/Waiwai_language" title="Waiwai language">Waiwai</a>, <a href="/wiki/Warao_language" title="Warao language">Warao</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Wayana_language" title="Wayana language">Wayana</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Hakka_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Hakka language">Hakka</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cantonese_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Cantonese language">Cantonese</a> are spoken by the descendants of the Chinese indentured labourers. <a href="/wiki/Standard_Mandarin" class="mw-redirect" title="Standard Mandarin">Mandarin</a> is spoken by the recent wave of Chinese immigrants.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/English_language" title="English language">English</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guyanese_Creole" title="Guyanese Creole">Guyanese English Creole</a>, <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language">Portuguese</a>, <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>, <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>, and <a href="/wiki/French_Guianese_Creole" title="French Guianese Creole">French Guianese Creole</a> are spoken at areas near the country's borders where there are many migrants from neighboring countries speaking their respective languages.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Largest_cities">Largest cities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Largest cities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The national capital, Paramaribo, is by far the dominant urban area, accounting for nearly half of Suriname's population and most of its urban residents. Indeed, <a href="/wiki/Primate_city" title="Primate city">its population is greater than the next nine largest cities combined</a>. Most municipalities are located within the capital's metropolitan area, or along the densely populated coastline.
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1148739982">.mw-parser-output .largestCities-table-background{background:#f9f9f9}.mw-parser-output .largestCities-cell-background{background:#f0f0f0}</style>
</p>
<div>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Suriname" title="Culture of Suriname">Culture of Suriname</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholicism_in_Suriname" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholicism in Suriname">Roman Catholicism in Suriname</a>, <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Suriname" title="Music of Suriname">Music of Suriname</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hinduism_in_Suriname" title="Hinduism in Suriname">Hinduism in Suriname</a>, <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Suriname" title="Islam in Suriname">Islam in Suriname</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Arya_Samaj_in_Suriname" title="Arya Samaj in Suriname">Arya Samaj in Suriname</a></div>
<p>Owing to the country's multicultural heritage, Suriname celebrates a variety of distinct ethnic and religious festivals.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="National_holidays">National holidays</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: National holidays">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>1 January – <a href="/wiki/New_Year%27s_Day" title="New Year&#39;s Day">New Year's Day</a></li>
<li>January/February – <a href="/wiki/Chinese_New_Year" title="Chinese New Year">Chinese New Year</a></li>
<li>March (varies) – <a href="/wiki/Holi" title="Holi">Phagwah</a></li>
<li>March/April – <a href="/wiki/Good_Friday" title="Good Friday">Good Friday</a></li>
<li>March/April – <a href="/wiki/Easter" title="Easter">Easter</a></li>
<li>March/April – <a href="/wiki/Easter_Monday" title="Easter Monday">Easter Monday</a></li>
<li>1 May – <a href="/wiki/Labour_Day" title="Labour Day">Labour Day</a></li>
<li>1 July – <a href="/wiki/Keti_Koti" class="mw-redirect" title="Keti Koti">Keti Koti</a> (<a href="/wiki/Emancipation_Day" title="Emancipation Day">Emancipation Day</a> – end of slavery)</li>
<li>9 August – <a href="/wiki/International_Day_of_the_World%27s_Indigenous_Peoples" title="International Day of the World&#39;s Indigenous Peoples">Indigenous People's Day</a></li>
<li>10 October – <a href="/wiki/Maroons" title="Maroons">Day of the Maroons</a></li>
<li>October/November – <a href="/wiki/Diwali" title="Diwali">Diwali</a></li>
<li>25 November – <a href="/wiki/Independence_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="Independence Day">Independence Day</a></li>
<li>25 December – <a href="/wiki/Christmas" title="Christmas">Christmas</a></li>
<li>26 December – <a href="/wiki/Boxing_Day" title="Boxing Day">Boxing Day</a></li>
<li>varies - <a href="/wiki/Eid-ul-adha" class="mw-redirect" title="Eid-ul-adha">Eid-ul-adha</a></li>
<li>varies - <a href="/wiki/Eid-ul-Fitr" class="mw-redirect" title="Eid-ul-Fitr">Eid-ul-Fitr</a></li>
<li>varies - <a href="/wiki/Satu_Suro" title="Satu Suro">Satu Suro</a>/<a href="/wiki/Islamic_New_Year" title="Islamic New Year">Islamic New Year</a><sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>There are several <a href="/wiki/Hindu" class="mw-redirect" title="Hindu">Hindu</a> and <a href="/wiki/Islamic" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamic">Islamic</a> national holidays like <a href="/wiki/Diwali" title="Diwali">Diwali</a>, <a href="/wiki/Phagwa" class="mw-redirect" title="Phagwa">Phagwa</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Eid-ul-adha" class="mw-redirect" title="Eid-ul-adha">Eid-ul-adha</a>. These holidays do not have fixed dates on the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_calendar" title="Gregorian calendar">Gregorian calendar</a>, as they are based on the <a href="/wiki/Hindu_calendar" title="Hindu calendar">Hindu</a> and <a href="/wiki/Islamic_calendar" title="Islamic calendar">Islamic calendars</a>, respectively. As of 2020, Eid-ul-adha is a national holiday, and equal to a Sunday.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are several holidays which are unique to Suriname. These include the <a href="/wiki/Prawas_Din" title="Prawas Din">Prawas Din</a> (<a href="/wiki/Indian_Arrival_Day" title="Indian Arrival Day">Indian</a>), Javanese, and Chinese arrival days. They celebrate the arrival of the first ships with their respective immigrants.
</p>
<h4><span id="New_Year.27s_Eve"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="New_Year's_Eve">New Year's Eve</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: New Year&#039;s Eve">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kerstoudjaar_037.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Kerstoudjaar_037.jpg/220px-Kerstoudjaar_037.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Kerstoudjaar_037.jpg/330px-Kerstoudjaar_037.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Kerstoudjaar_037.jpg/440px-Kerstoudjaar_037.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Pagara (red firecracker ribbons)</figcaption></figure>
<p>New Year's Eve in Suriname is called <i>Oud jaar</i>, <i>Owru Yari</i>, or "old year". <a href="/wiki/Firecrackers" class="mw-redirect" title="Firecrackers">Firecrackers</a> called <i>pagaras</i> which have long ribbons attached and are detonated at midnight.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports">Sports</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Sports">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The major sports in Suriname are <a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">football</a>, <a href="/wiki/Basketball" title="Basketball">basketball</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Volleyball" title="Volleyball">volleyball</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Suriname_Olympic_Committee" title="Suriname Olympic Committee">Suriname Olympic Committee</a> is the national governing body for sports in Suriname. The major <a href="/wiki/Mind_sport" title="Mind sport">mind sports</a> are <a href="/wiki/Chess" title="Chess">chess</a>, <a href="/wiki/Draughts" class="mw-redirect" title="Draughts">draughts</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bridge_(card_game)" class="mw-redirect" title="Bridge (card game)">bridge</a> and <a href="/wiki/Troefcall" title="Troefcall">troefcall</a>.
</p><p>Many Suriname-born football players and Dutch-born football players of Surinamese descent have turned out to play for the <a href="/wiki/Netherlands_national_football_team" title="Netherlands national football team">Dutch national team</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Gerald_Vanenburg" title="Gerald Vanenburg">Gerald Vanenburg</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ruud_Gullit" title="Ruud Gullit">Ruud Gullit</a>, <a href="/wiki/Frank_Rijkaard" title="Frank Rijkaard">Frank Rijkaard</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edgar_Davids" title="Edgar Davids">Edgar Davids</a>, <a href="/wiki/Clarence_Seedorf" title="Clarence Seedorf">Clarence Seedorf</a>, <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Kluivert" title="Patrick Kluivert">Patrick Kluivert</a>, <a href="/wiki/Aron_Winter" title="Aron Winter">Aron Winter</a>, <a href="/wiki/Georginio_Wijnaldum" title="Georginio Wijnaldum">Georginio Wijnaldum</a>, <a href="/wiki/Virgil_van_Dijk" title="Virgil van Dijk">Virgil van Dijk</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Jimmy_Floyd_Hasselbaink" title="Jimmy Floyd Hasselbaink">Jimmy Floyd Hasselbaink</a>. In 1999, <a href="/wiki/Humphrey_Mijnals" title="Humphrey Mijnals">Humphrey Mijnals</a>, who played for both <a href="/wiki/Suriname_national_football_team" title="Suriname national football team">Suriname</a> and the Netherlands, was elected Surinamese footballer of the century.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Another famous player is <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Kamperveen" title="André Kamperveen">André Kamperveen</a>, who captained Suriname in the 1940s and was the first Surinamese to play professionally in the Netherlands.
</p><p>Swimmer <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Nesty" title="Anthony Nesty">Anthony Nesty</a> is the only <a href="/wiki/Olympic_Games" title="Olympic Games">Olympic</a> medalist for Suriname. He won gold in the 100-meter butterfly at the <a href="/wiki/1988_Summer_Olympics" title="1988 Summer Olympics">1988 Summer Olympics</a> in <a href="/wiki/Seoul" title="Seoul">Seoul</a> and he won bronze in the same discipline at the 1992 Summer Olympics in <a href="/wiki/Barcelona" title="Barcelona">Barcelona</a>. Originally from <a href="/wiki/Trinidad_and_Tobago" title="Trinidad and Tobago">Trinidad and Tobago</a>, he now lives in <a href="/wiki/Gainesville,_Florida" title="Gainesville, Florida">Gainesville, Florida</a>, and is the head coach of the University of Florida swim team.
</p><p>The most famous international track &amp; field athlete from Suriname is <a href="/wiki/Letitia_Vriesde" title="Letitia Vriesde">Letitia Vriesde</a>, who won a silver medal at the <a href="/wiki/1995_World_Championships_in_Athletics" title="1995 World Championships in Athletics">1995 World Championships</a> behind Ana Quirot in the 800 metres, the first medal won by a South American female athlete in World Championship competition. In addition, she also won a bronze medal at the <a href="/wiki/2001_World_Championships_in_Athletics" title="2001 World Championships in Athletics">2001 World Championships</a> and won several medals in the 800 and 1500 metres at the <a href="/wiki/Pan-American_Games" class="mw-redirect" title="Pan-American Games">Pan-American Games</a> and <a href="/wiki/Central_American_and_Caribbean_Games" title="Central American and Caribbean Games">Central American and Caribbean Games</a>. <a href="/wiki/Tommy_Asinga" title="Tommy Asinga">Tommy Asinga</a> also received acclaim for winning a bronze medal in the 800 metres at the <a href="/wiki/1991_Pan_American_Games" title="1991 Pan American Games">1991 Pan American Games</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Cricket" title="Cricket">Cricket</a> is popular in Suriname to some extent, influenced by its popularity <a href="/wiki/Cricket_in_the_Netherlands" title="Cricket in the Netherlands">in the Netherlands</a> and in neighbouring Guyana. The <a href="/wiki/Surinaamse_Cricket_Bond" class="mw-redirect" title="Surinaamse Cricket Bond">Surinaamse Cricket Bond</a> is an <a href="/wiki/List_of_International_Cricket_Council_members" title="List of International Cricket Council members">associate member</a> of the <a href="/wiki/International_Cricket_Council" title="International Cricket Council">International Cricket Council</a> (ICC). Suriname and Argentina were the only ICC associate members in South America when ICC had a three tiered membership, although Guyana is represented on the <a href="/wiki/West_Indies_Cricket_Board" class="mw-redirect" title="West Indies Cricket Board">West Indies Cricket Board</a>, a full member. <a href="/wiki/Suriname_national_cricket_team" title="Suriname national cricket team">The national cricket team</a> was ranked 47th in the world and sixth in the <a href="/wiki/ICC_Americas" title="ICC Americas">ICC Americas</a> region as of June 2014, and competes in the <a href="/wiki/World_Cricket_League" title="World Cricket League">World Cricket League</a> (WCL) and <a href="/wiki/ICC_Americas_Championship" title="ICC Americas Championship">ICC Americas Championship</a>. <a href="/wiki/Iris_Jharap" title="Iris Jharap">Iris Jharap</a>, born in Paramaribo, played <a href="/wiki/Women%27s_One_Day_International" title="Women&#39;s One Day International">women's One Day International</a> matches for <a href="/wiki/Netherlands_national_women%27s_cricket_team" class="mw-redirect" title="Netherlands national women&#39;s cricket team">the Dutch national side</a>, the only Surinamese to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the sport of <a href="/wiki/Badminton" title="Badminton">badminton</a>, another popular sport in Suriname especially with the youth, the local heroes are <a href="/wiki/Virgil_Soeroredjo" title="Virgil Soeroredjo">Virgil Soeroredjo</a>,   <a href="/wiki/Mitchel_Wongsodikromo" title="Mitchel Wongsodikromo">Mitchel Wongsodikromo</a>, <a href="/wiki/S%C3%B6ren_Opti" title="Sören Opti">Sören Opti</a> and also <a href="/wiki/Crystal_Leefmans" title="Crystal Leefmans">Crystal Leefmans</a>. All winning medals for Suriname at the <a href="/wiki/Carebaco_International" title="Carebaco International">Carebaco Caribbean Championships</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Central_American_and_Caribbean_Games" title="Central American and Caribbean Games">Central American and Caribbean Games</a> (CACSO Games)<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> and also at the <a href="/wiki/South_American_Games" title="South American Games">South American Games</a>, better known as the <a href="/wiki/ODESUR" title="ODESUR">ODESUR</a> Games. Virgil Soeroredjo also participated for Suriname at the <a href="/wiki/Badminton_at_the_2012_Summer_Olympics" title="Badminton at the 2012 Summer Olympics">2012 London Summer Olympics</a>, only the second badminton player, after <a href="/wiki/Oscar_Brandon" title="Oscar Brandon">Oscar Brandon</a>, for Suriname to achieve this.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Surinamese_National_Badminton_Championships" title="Surinamese National Badminton Championships">National Champion</a> <a href="/wiki/S%C3%B6ren_Opti" title="Sören Opti">Sören Opti</a> became the third Surinamese badminton player to participate at the <a href="/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics" title="2016 Summer Olympics">Summer Olympics</a> in 2016.
</p><p>Multiple time <a href="/wiki/K-1" title="K-1">K-1</a> <a href="/wiki/Kickboxing" title="Kickboxing">kickboxing</a> world champions <a href="/wiki/Ernesto_Hoost" title="Ernesto Hoost">Ernesto Hoost</a> and <a href="/wiki/Remy_Bonjasky" title="Remy Bonjasky">Remy Bonjasky</a> were born in Suriname or are of Surinamese descent. Other kickboxing world champions include <a href="/wiki/Rayen_Simson" title="Rayen Simson">Rayen Simson</a>, <a href="/wiki/Melvin_Manhoef" title="Melvin Manhoef">Melvin Manhoef</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tyrone_Spong" title="Tyrone Spong">Tyrone Spong</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jairzinho_Rozenstruik" title="Jairzinho Rozenstruik">Jairzinho Rozenstruik</a>, <a href="/wiki/Regian_Eersel" title="Regian Eersel">Regian Eersel</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Donovan_Wisse" title="Donovan Wisse">Donovan Wisse</a>.
</p><p>Suriname also has a <a href="/wiki/Suriname_national_korfball_team" title="Suriname national korfball team">national korfball team</a>, with <a href="/wiki/Korfball" title="Korfball">korfball</a> being a Dutch sport. <a href="/wiki/Vinkensport" title="Vinkensport">Vinkensport</a> is also practised.
</p><p>In 2016, the <a href="/wiki/Sports_Hall_of_Fame_Suriname" title="Sports Hall of Fame Suriname">Sports Hall of Fame Suriname</a> was established in the building of the Suriname Olympic Committee and is dedicated to the achievements of the Surinamese sporters.<sup id="cite_ref-SN_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SN-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Transportation">Transportation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Transportation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Transport_in_Suriname" title="Transport in Suriname">Transport in Suriname</a>, <a href="/wiki/Desir%C3%A9_Delano_Bouterse_Highway" title="Desiré Delano Bouterse Highway">Desiré Delano Bouterse Highway</a>, and <a href="/wiki/East-West_Link_(Suriname)" title="East-West Link (Suriname)">East-West Link (Suriname)</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Road">Road</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Road">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Suriname, along with neighboring <a href="/wiki/Guyana" title="Guyana">Guyana</a>, is one of only two countries on the mainland South American continent that drive on the left, although many vehicles are left-hand-drive as well as right-hand-drive.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> One explanation for this practice is that at the time of its colonization of Suriname, the Netherlands itself used left-hand traffic, also introducing the practice in the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_East_Indies" title="Dutch East Indies">Dutch East Indies</a>, now <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-New_Scientist_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-New_Scientist-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Another is that Suriname was first colonized by the British, and for practical reasons, this was not changed when it came under Dutch administration.<sup id="cite_ref-Kincaid_Rule_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kincaid_Rule-114">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Although the Netherlands converted to driving to the right at the end of the 18th century,<sup id="cite_ref-New_Scientist_113-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-New_Scientist-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Suriname did not.
As of 2003, Suriname had 4303&#160;km (2674 miles) of roads, of which 1119&#160;km (695 miles) are paved.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Air">Air</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Suriname&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Air">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The country has 55 mostly small airports,<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> of which only six are paved. 
The only international airport that supports large <a href="/wiki/Jet_aircraft" title="Jet aircraft">jet aircraft</a> is <a href="/wiki/Johan_Adolf_Pengel_International_Airport" title="Johan Adolf Pengel International Airport">Johan Adolf Pengel International Airport</a>.
</p><p>Airlines with departures from Suriname:
</p>
<ul><li>American Airlines<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Blue_Wing_Airlines" title="Blue Wing Airlines">Blue Wing Airlines</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Gum_Air" title="Gum Air">Gum Air</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Fly_All_Ways" title="Fly All Ways">Fly All Ways</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Surinam_Airways" title="Surinam Airways">Surinam Airways</a> <i>(SLM)</i></li></ul>
<p>Airlines with arrivals in Suriname:
</p>
